ParlaMint-EE_2021-06-02_U4	Thank you, Mr. Chairman. Prime Minister! The government intends to set up a task force to fight anti-Semitism to protect the Jewish community. There has never been persecution of Jews or other nations in Estonia. Before the war, Estonia was transferred to the so-called Jewish golden book, because unlike many countries they had no problems here. If so, you can also see this witness at the museum near the synagogue. The Jews were granted cultural autonomy, had all rights compared to other citizens, and were not discriminated against. The same is true in Estonia today. They are just as respected and equal citizens as everyone else. According to the Police and Border Guard Board, there is no anti-Semitism or anti-Semitic crimes in Estonia. Why, then, should the government distinguish them from other nations here, and from whom should they still be protected? Does the government really think that the Estonian people, who have suffered in the same way as the Jews in history, have any evil plans against them? By setting up such a working group, the government sends a signal to the world, as if there were a problem. I ask: why do you create a non-existent problem, ruin Estonia's good reputation, and what does all this cost our taxpayer?

ParlaMint-EE_2021-06-02_U18	Maris Lauri, please!
ParlaMint-EE_2021-06-02_U19	Thank you. This is certainly an extremely important subject to be discussed. The proposals that were made there and sent back to the coordination circle and have now come back with the response are indeed varied, because the parties have very different views on the matter. Perhaps the figurative example is that when this draft was sent to coordination, I received two responses in the hours I had read the same day, and they were contradictory. So it is certainly difficult to find a compromise. What's the problem? The problem is that we have a minimum living with regard to the minimum wage and the minimum wage has risen extremely quickly, so the payment of a living has become very burdensome for some people. This ends, for example, with children getting nothing, even when parents are willing to pay, because the parent is simply not able to pay, or when younger children of another family are in difficult circumstances who are unable to receive a living. We need to find a balance there now. One of the changes, which is currently very much discussed, is a transitional provision. It seems to me that this principle, which is in the draft, how to calculate the minimum in the future, is that it is a minimum of living that can always be increased through negotiations, is how to implement it. The positions here are contradictory. The Supreme Court and the judges indicate that we must monitor justice in one direction. The Chancellor of Justice points out that justice is also a matter of another direction. That's where the arguments are.

Yes, it was a legal debate in which the Chancellor of Justice is on the other side, who points to exactly the same thing as the official also pointed out that there is an obstacle to the existence of a judgment. So, between these two positions, a compromise must now be found on how to make this transitional provision. I expect and hope that the parties involved will sit down together and, since he is primarily a legal matter, will discuss it thoroughly and find a suitable solution, which will be recognised by all the top specialists of our law as acceptable under the Constitution.

ParlaMint-EE_2021-06-02_U154	Mr President, I would like to thank you very much. Ladies and gentlemen, I will repeat the main points that I have already made on this draft at second reading. The area of activity or competence of the Emergency Act should not be extended to prepare for threats to national security and constitutional order. This is clearly a matter of emergency law. As security authorities are also preparing for this on a daily basis in normal circumstances, it would in principle provide an opportunity for arbitrary interpretations and implementation under normal circumstances. That, however, should not be our goal. The second substantive problem concerns governance. The Government of the Republic, as a collective body, could not coordinate crisis management; this can be done as one particular person by the Prime Minister. But on the basis of a collective body, this solution would not work. The coordination of crisis management should also not be transferred from the Ministry of the Interior to the State Chancellery. It should stay the way it's been so far. The Ministry of the Interior is the only ministry that has the actual capacity to coordinate and manage the resolution of domestic crises. Unfortunately, it was not used in the autumn of last year and in the spring of this year. However, this does not mean that it will not work or that this role needs to be given to someone else. The State Chancellery does not have the capacity, structure or practical experience to perform this role successfully and the creation of such capacity would require the State Chancellery to carry out a ministry-like institution with all that is now necessarily to take place. Despite the positive aspects I have highlighted, these two reasons make it impossible for the Conservative People's Party of Estonia to support this draft. That's all. Thank you!

ParlaMint-EE_2021-06-02_U157	Please, eight minutes!
ParlaMint-EE_2021-06-02_U158	Dear representative of the people, well, here we are! The Fatherland cannot support this bill in this form. But this law is a complex, it has a number of independent circles of topics put together. As the good previous speaker mentioned, this is actually about crisis management. For us, there are three fundamental questions here which, according to this logic, are not appropriate to be dealt with. The first is that we must draw conclusions from the corona crisis. Parliament's proceedings were given a very large set of national defence laws during the previous government, several hundred pages of the explanatory memorandum and hundreds of sections of the law itself, and it provided for the creation of a single crisis management model for the whole country. Now, however, a paragraph has been torn out of it, and this paragraph is to be applied without the context in which it was located. We all know that in Parliament's Committee on National Defence there was a huge controversy and debate on this whole approach, there were different proposals, but now we are trying to solve the problem of crisis management or preparation for crisis policy through one section at a 'corner' point. And what is really problematic is this one paragraph, which concerns the collegial responsibility of the government, which would be operationally furnished by the State Chancellery. In fact, there is only one sentence in this law and it can be interpreted in a very different way if there is no such context as the National Defence Act. There must be clarity in the management of crises, but unfortunately this draft law is breaking down. This is not relevant. And more than that. Representatives of the State Chancellery, officials, assured the Committee on Constitutional Affairs that, before this law is passed and the ink can be dried up on paper, the government intends to come up with a new package already in June, which in reality should change the whole of this crepe. Maybe one trolleybus can't even get out of the depot when the other one comes in and it's all happening in a situation, respectable learned friends, where we still have a corona crisis. In the midst of a crisis management system to change our position, it is not appropriate. We need to learn from this crisis, some things were very well, some things need to change the system, there is no doubt about that. But it is clear that this cannot be done with one section in the middle of a crisis according to the logic that we are beginning to build the next draft law in this context. Thirdly, it is certainly a question for us about the transfer of competence to the State Chancellery. In our opinion, this certainly deserves to be considered and considered. In the context of all these circumstances, there is no doubt about the stronger enthusiasts in this country in matters of national defence and security than the friendly and always constructive team of the Fatherland. But in the current situation, we see that the concept of a single section, glued together, out of the whole package of the National Defence Act, does not really serve the systematic objective of clarifying the chain of command and more effective crisis management that we would like to see. So there's nothing catastrophic about this. It makes sense for us to take time off, not to support this law in this form, to remove those provisions that deal with stocks, and to adopt these provisions in a nice way. And these other issues could be dealt with in a holistic way in a consensual mind, as should the organisation of the security of a small, independent defence capacity and of a country wishing to counter external and internal threats. The Fatherland says yes to effective systemic crisis response. The Fatherland says yes to this system which provides for the establishment of a national reserve agency. However, we certainly do not say yes to the restructuring of the system-to-work crisis management by lifting one section out of the whole large-scale systemic package, which had been counted months ago on Parliament's table as a comprehensive solution to national defence law. Thank you.

ParlaMint-EE_2021-06-02_U171	We will move on to today's second item on the agenda: Indrek Saare, Keit Pentus-Rosimannus, Jürgen Ligi, Signe Riisalo, Yoko Alenderi, Helmen Küti, Lauri Läänemetsa, Siret Kotka, Andrei Korobeinik, Raimond Kaljulaid, Oudekki Loone, Katri Raigi, Jüri Jaansoni, Toomas Kivimägi, Johannes Kerdi, Mart Võrklaat, Jevgeni Ossinovski, Kalvi Kuld, Jaak Juske, Riina Skikutti, Heljo Pikhof, Liina Kersna and Madis Milling, third reading of the draft law amending the Law on Animal Protection and Nature Conservation Act 219. I'm opening negotiations. On behalf of the Yoko Alender Reform Party Group, please! Ladies and gentlemen, please! [[It's ringing the bell.]]
ParlaMint-EE_2021-06-02_U172	Ladies and gentlemen, Mr President, I would like to thank you very much. First of all, animal welfare problems in fur farms can't be overlooked. They mainly grow foxes, mink and chinchillas. There are still chinchillas and foxes left in Estonia, but several hundred thousand minks were also present on our farms a few years ago. The report on animal welfare in fur farms commissioned by the European Commission highlights a number of intrinsic concerns about the housing of animals in fur farms that cannot be resolved without the industry becoming economically non-profitable. In the case of minks, for example, the current regulation provides that there must be room for 0.25 square metres per mink in the cage. The bottom of the cage, or floor, is wired to allow animal droppings to fall directly onto the ground. The minks, as semi-aquatic animals, do not have any opportunity to swim during their lifetime. On the basis of the European Commission's survey on fur animals, such conditions are detrimental to the well-being of mink, as they do not provide an opportunity to meet important needs. The most serious concerns are limited mobility and lack of action. The European Commission's report also points out that these animals, which live in the wild, do not have the opportunity to avoid contact with hundreds of thousands of other animals on fur farms, causing aggression, self-harm or attacking and stress on their cage mates. In the case of foxes, the regulation in force in Estonia lays down 0.8 square metres per animal. In the case of chinchillas, the lack of opportunities for movement and action is also a major concern, like foxes and minks. In addition, chinchillas are very good jumpers; they can jump up to two metres high. The cages on the bear farm are considerably smaller and can therefore cause injury to them. The chinchilla cage is 40 centimeters high. For countries where no road to ban fur farms has taken place, but the requirements for minks to have several square metres of clearance space and swimming pool (e.g. in Italy) have been abolished, as ensuring such conditions would make fur production in farms economically non-profitable. Indeed, more has been said about animal welfare in recent years here too, and this is a good sign because we have the opportunity to pay attention to even the weakest in society, who cannot stand up for themselves and alleviate their own suffering. During these ten years, when the ban on fur farms has been discussed in Estonia, today this is the third time in the Riigikogu, the market has been able to make its own corrections and the livestock farming industry is about to cease its activities. In 2015, there were 75 jobs in the sector and around 200000 animals. Today there are less than 1000 animals and 11 workers. From this it can be concluded that the sector has virtually decreased by 99% over a decade. According to March, the animals are kept on four small chinchilla farms and one fox farm. The largest fur farm in Estonia, once located in Karjaküla, owned by Finns, the last animals in leather at the end of last year, and did not continue the new production cycle due to the unfavourable situation on the market. Several factors have affected this major decline. On the one hand, the global fur market is at a great low; on the other hand, prices are low in auctions. There was also a great deal of unsold skin due to the Corona virus, with reduced income. Public opinion has also changed a great deal. With the development of consumer awareness and responsible entrepreneurship, a large number of consumers in the fashion industry and many leading fashion companies have also abandoned the use of fur from fur farms. The market situation has also been affected by the increase in the number of countries that have banned fur farms. More time, please!
ParlaMint-EE_2021-06-02_U173	Please, three more minutes!
ParlaMint-EE_2021-06-02_U174	These countries include, for example, Great Britain, Norway, the Netherlands, Austria, Croatia, Slovenia, Macedonia, Serbia, Bosnia and Herzegovina, Slovakia, Luxembourg, Belgium, the Czech Republic, France and Ireland. Fox farms have been banned in Denmark and Sweden, and in many countries the political debate about banning is still underway, such as Poland, Lithuania, Ukraine and Bulgaria. In Finland, too, the ban on fur farms is one of the points of the debate on major innovations in the Animal Protection Act this spring. Now for public opinion. The results of public opinion studies have shown a gradual increase in the support of Estonian residents for this draft and the ban on fur farms. The latest results were obtained from a survey conducted by Kantar Emor in March this year asking whether the cultivation and execution of foxes, minks and chinchillas for fur purposes should be allowed in Estonia. The result was that 75% of the Estonian population felt that this should not be allowed. And actually, if you look at these research results from different social groups, you can see that support is high among all party supporters and among both urban and rural residents. In many countries, it is the leaders of the ban that have a conservative view of the world, and in other countries it is the opposite, the liberals. I would also like to stress that we are also dealing with the initiative of members of the Riigikogu, not a draft government. Many interest groups have also launched petitions, the last of which received the necessary 1000 signatures of support in less than a day. This same petition was also the impetus for the draft that came to third reading today. Supporting this draft is an opportunity to show support for a highly supported civil society initiative. As a result of the Commission's work, an amendment has been introduced which will ensure a transitional period of five years so that the 11 people [currently in this sector] who are occupied can find new challenges and move forward in other areas. The majority of the Reform Party Group supports this draft and I call on you to do the same. Thank you.

ParlaMint-EE_2021-06-02_U191	Let us move on to the third item on today's agenda. For this purpose, the second reading of the draft amendment to the Riigikogu decision "Constitution of a Problem Committee on the Development of Estonian Language Learning" submitted by the Group of the Estonian Reform Party and the Group of the Estonian Centre Party has been submitted. ParlaMint-EE_2021-06-02_U192	Ladies and gentlemen, This bill was passed at first reading in Riigikogu plenary on 10 March. The first reading of the draft was closed and the deadline for submitting amendments was set at 25 March. A total of 270 amendments to the draft were tabled by the deadline. A total of 214 amendments were tabled by five groups of members of the EKRE Group and 56 amendments were tabled by the Members of the Isamaa Group. The Committee on Constitutional Affairs prepared the draft for a second reading at its meeting on 18 May. The chairman of the committee, Toomas Kivimägi, acknowledged all the political groups of the Riigikogu for their constructive negotiations and mentioned that all political parties were prepared to seek a compromise in order to find a form of organisation that would be acceptable to everyone in the case of special committees. Mr Kivimägi pointed out that all the amendments had been withdrawn by 18 May, during the meeting of the Commission, and this move was conditional on the agreement between all the political groups that the draft decision stipulates that the problem committee on the development of the Estonian language is to be formed on the basis of the principle of equal representation of the coalition and opposition, and consequently the Committee on Constitutional Affairs may propose a corresponding amendment to the draft decision. Mr Kivimägi also mentioned that in the interests of better regulation, an amendment should also be made concerning the date of entry into force of the draft decision. The draft list of amendments contained two amendments. The first of these reworded the decision as follows: "To set up a Committee on Problems in the Development of Estonian Language Learning. The Commission shall be formed on the basis of the principle of equal representation between the coalition and the opposition. " The Commission decided to present and support this proposal. It was voted on. There were 9 members in favour of the proposal, no one opposed, and no abstentions. Peeter Ernits, Toomas Kivimägi, Andrei Korobeinik, Oudekki Loone, Lauri Läänemets, Paul Puustusmaa, Urmas Reinsalu, Marko Storm and Mart Võrguship voted in favour. Amendment 2 was to delete point 2: "This decision will enter into force when it is signed. "The reason for this was that since the Riigikogu Law on Home and Rules of Procedure provides that the decision of the Riigikogu will enter into force by signing, the text of the law will no longer need to be repeated in the decision. If we'd like a second deadline, it should be written down. The Commission also decided to present this proposal in a consensual manner. There were nine members in favour, all the same ones whose names I already read out. We then made procedural decisions. It was decided to propose that the draft be placed on the plenary agenda by 2 June 2021, by consensus, and to propose that the second reading of the draft be terminated and that the final vote on the draft be carried out. This decision was also made by consensus. Thank you very much.

I do not know if the Prime Minister has considered the proposal and has responded to Mr Jaak Uibu. It's not the responsibility of one minister or ministry. That is why we are in favour of setting up a population office with the Prime Minister, which would coordinate and be responsible for the demise of the population in a cross-ministerial way, working together with the Chancellors of Ministries and Deputy Chancellors, top specialists in the fields and local authorities. It is precisely responsibility that needs to be specified for the tasks and responsibilities of ministries and local authorities in the exercise of population policy. Individual measures do not produce results because there is a wholesale dependency on the population issue. In order to increase the birth rate, material support is not enough. Population processes depend on education, living environment, mental, social, physical, psychic and much more. The barriers to birth rates must therefore be reduced and birth rates must be created. In addition to the forward-looking and planned work, we also consider it very important to pay attention to the health, education and living environment of today's families and growing children. People's health and birth rates are inseparable. It is possible to change the factors of birth rate, health, life and relationships. Population scientist Kalev Katus has said that the development of society must be addressed in a population-centred way. Estonia is in a population crisis, meaning the demographic state of the population, characterised by a lack of internal recovery potential and an ageing population. However, I would like to conclude more positively with the words of President Lennart Meri, which have not lost their topicality today. ParlaMint-EE_2021-06-02_U286	We move on to the sixth item, this is the first reading of the draft Act 385 on the ratification of the Agreement between the Government of the Republic of Estonia and the Republic of Mauritius on the prevention of double taxation and on the prevention of tax evasion and fraud and related protocol. I would like to ask the Minister of Finance Keit Pentus-Rosimitud to serve as a rapporteur for the Riigikogu's speech chair. Please!
ParlaMint-EE_2021-06-02_U287	Mr President of the Riigikogu. Happy Members of the Riigikogu! The Government of the Republic has submitted to the Riigikogu a double taxation convention between Estonia and Mauritius for ratification. As everyone knows, Estonia is still concluding double taxation agreements to ensure the stability of the tax environment both for those who invest in Estonia and for Estonian entrepreneurs who extend their activities to foreign markets. The aim of tax treaties is to eliminate double international taxation, which may arise in combination with the laws of two countries. The agreement divides tax rights between the parties to the agreement, avoids discriminatory taxation and, of course, prevents tax evasion. The agreement does not provide additional tax opportunities for the parties but limits the existing tax rights established by national law. This tax agreement applies to persons who are residents of either Estonia or Mauritius. The agreement before you was signed, including on 19 February in Brussels, and is based on the OECD Standard Agreement. If you have any questions about this agreement, I would be happy to answer, but I would ask you for your support in ratifying it. Thank you.
ParlaMint-EE_2021-06-02_U288	Minister, it seems that there are no questions. Thank you. Now I ask Dmitri Dmitriyev, member of the Monetary Committee, to present the speech chair of the Riigikogu.

ParlaMint-EE_2021-06-02_U291	We will move on to the seventh item, which is the first reading of the draft law amending the Aliens Act, the Law on Higher Education and the Law on Educational Grants and Student Loan, initiated by the Group of the Conservative People's Party of Estonia. I am very pleased to ask a member of the Riigikogu Jaak Valge to present the speech chair here.
ParlaMint-EE_2021-06-02_U292	Ladies and gentlemen, I am also very happy to present here and present this bill. I am not going to rewrite this bill to you, but rather to talk about the situation that led to the bill. Let me start with the basics first. You see, an immigration policy based on society's needs is one of the country's most important functions. As we know, immigration is beneficial for the state if its volume is reasonable and migratory people who quickly find their place in the new society. Otherwise, this process will be harmful to society, in fact, harmful to society and harmful to immigrants themselves, at least in the long term. Our Estonia's migration story is, let's say, the dynamics of volumes developed in a similar way to that of other Europe, perhaps some ups and downs have been sharper. One exception, with a very large exception, has been the period of occupation when we ourselves were not able to regulate immigration. The proportion of people of foreign origin is still the highest in Europe due to the large immigration in the period of occupation. We have an outside birth rate of around 200,000. And given the second generation's external population, which it would be reasonable to do when it comes to integration, we know that this figure is higher than the highest in Europe, only in Luxembourg. This means that we are dealing with one of the most difficult, difficult problems of European integration. This should lead us to conduct an extraordinarily cautious immigration policy. And indeed, for the time being, that was the case. But since 2015, net migration has been so high that the share of Estonians in Estonia has started to decline again. It's true, very slowly. However, a new phenomenon has emerged since 2018: immigration from third countries, i.e. from non-EU countries, has increased. In our case, this means countries in the eastern Slavic cultural space, but on the other hand, countries are very different, mainly from Asia and Africa. The current 11000 Ukrainian citizens' community in Estonia is now growing very rapidly. In 2020, or last year, we knew we had corona restrictions, but there were still just over 16,000 immigrants, just over 12,000 emigrants. These include short-term workers not included. And now I recall that, in restoring independence, we set the immigration limit at 0.1% of the permanent population in one year. Let's do a small calculation: in 2020, last year, despite the corona restrictions, 12 times more people migrated than we thought when we created our country. Look at how, in a few decades, our attitudes have changed radically if you take that number! Politics should, like, reflect attitudes. I'm saying it doesn't. In 2019, i.e. the last normal year, the main reasons for obtaining a residence permit in Estonia were coming to work, going to study and family migration. After all, family migration makes sense because family members come to live in Estonia, who come here to study and work. In general, we should take into account that there are two family members coming to work or study for each third person. This is the case with the current statistics, and this figure will definitely be higher later. It's projected to be bigger. The number of family members accompanying foreign students is not yet very high, but it has almost doubled every year. There is no need to be a prophet at all, to say that it will probably double if we do not change the circumstances. Now, in 2017, the possibilities for family members to settle in Estonia were significantly expanded. And there was another negative fact last year. While some time ago we rejoiced that the return of Estonian citizens to Estonia was larger than the emigration from Estonia, last year the return of Estonian citizens was less than 1000. Unfortunately, our migration data does not shine with precision, and it is too early to draw comprehensive conclusions, but in any case it is clearly an alarming very negative trend. And all in all, it's clear that we need to look for solutions. Now let's look at learning mobility first. In this normal year, 2019, 1,300 persons received a primary residence permit in Estonia, including Nigerian, Russian, Ukrainian, Bangladeshi, India, Azerbaijani and Pakistani citizens. I would also stress that, without taking into account, these are foreign students from the European Union and EFTA countries. Of all these foreign students, 5% or slightly less than 5% studied in Estonian, but about half of them worked during their studies. There are no or at least no data on employment for third-country students. However, they are expected to have a sharply higher share of work. All right, let's keep looking. Doctoral studies are free of charge for foreign students. 66% of students from third countries paid tuition fees in the first and second stage, while others did not. This is 89% for students from the European Union and EFTA countries. Students from third countries paid much less. It should be borne in mind that, at least in public universities, tuition costs are not actually covered. Thus, Estonian taxpayers are also paid by foreign students who actually pay tuition fees to the university. In addition, about a third of foreign students are also paid needs-based learning support by the Estonian taxpayer, and almost all need-based learners come from so-called third countries. Let us admit that it is not easy to control whether or not they need to receive needs-based education support. In other words, it cannot be controlled. After graduation, only part of foreign students remain permanent residents in Estonia. Jaak Aaviksoo has noted that the reason for many foreign students to come to Estonia is the desire to move elsewhere in the European Union. We cannot distinguish between so-called third-country students and I do not have any more recent data. However, considering all foreign students and full-time employees in Estonia, only 26% of the graduates in 2016 remained in Estonia on average. The higher share was for engineering, ICT and manufacturing and processing. I admit that it could be assumed that this proportion has increased in recent years and that the result here will certainly be two-sided estimates of more if we also take account of the so-called "job breaks" or "one-off jobs." But anyway, this means that preparing one foreign student for the Estonian labour market at Estonian universities costs our taxpayer much more than preparing an Estonian student. Further: The average working income in Estonia of foreigners graduating from higher education is lower for local graduates, for example, it is 18% lower for graduates of master's studies. There's nothing weird about that. If, during the course of the studio, the official language has not been learned, it must be supplemented by an army of small and medium-paid workers. Of course, in the case of doctoral graduates who have acquired specific advanced skills, the pay gap is much lower. In summary, it must be concluded that the fact that foreign students are currently studying in English is not in the interests of the Estonian state or of a large part of foreign students themselves, as much of the preparation required for Estonia is simply not available. That's all I'm going to focus on. But in general terms, it could be said that the reception needs to focus more on certain specialties, such as technology and ICT, and, of course, to dramatically increase learning in Estonian and to exclude coming to Estonia solely for study grants, as a stepping-stone for going to more prosperous countries. Then let's look at the people who come to Estonia for work. More than 2200 residence permits were issued in 2019, more than half of them to Ukrainian citizens, followed by Russia, Belarus and India. In 2017, when the Minister of the Interior was the Socialist Andres Anvelt, the conditions for coming to work in Estonia were relaxed. The most important was the reduction of the wage bill from 1.24 on average to 1.0 on average wages, also for short-term workers. The consequence was that Estonia became attractive to low- and medium-skilled workers. However, a more important change was the rapid increase in the number of short-term employees. In 2019, registration of short-term workers had increased more than four times since two years ago: more than 25000 of the registered workers accounted for only 4% of those who could be regarded as highly qualified workers on 1 June 2020. Three quarters of short-term workers come from Ukraine, the rest from Belarus, Russia, Moldova and Uzbekistan, and I read again in order of importance. Short-term work in itself is a stepping stone to becoming a permanent resident in Estonia. Along with the increase in the number of short-term workers from Ukraine, employment and family migration in Ukraine have also increased. The employment or permanent residence of low- and medium-skilled workers in Estonia suppresses our wage levels and hinders innovation in the economy. The address made on 5 May this year by the Confederation of Trade Unions requires that employers should primarily use the local labour force of Estonia in order to ensure the competitiveness and sustainability of the Estonian economy. This is the appeal to the Union of Trade Unions. I am asking here where are the Social Democrats who should stand up for our workers. Indeed, as of 1 May 2021, we have nearly 15000 valid registrations of short-term workers, but at the same time, according to the unemployment fund, we have 54000 unemployed and 5800 vacancies. It tends not to be the right thing to say that we have a shortage of labour in simple-work areas, because more than half of the jobseekers are well-paid or middle-paid, the same people who would claim to be in positions that have started to be filled by foreign workers. And now I ask the rhetorical question: is it strange in this situation that the return migration to Estonia has slowed down, is it going to leave again? I recall that our labour productivity is only 74% of the European Union average. If we continue to bring in cheap labour that suppresses our wages, our economy will focus on those sectors with cheap labour. This is slowing down innovation. Indeed, I do not want to overdramatise, but I dare say that in this case only dreams can be seen from an increase in productivity in our work to 110% of the European average, as stated in the Estonian 2035 strategy. So far, our immigrant population is mainly from countries that are culturally close to us, and compared to Finland and Sweden, for example, these people are quite well adapted to working life here. Once again, however, we ask whether immigration from eastern Slavic and African and Asian countries will help us to avoid social problems linked to the ageing of the population. Here's what I'm gonna say. The birth rate of the population of foreign origin from the Eastern Slavic Cultural Space follows the country of origin level and is much smaller than that of Estonians. Thus, the continuation of immigration of people from the Eastern Slavic Cultural Space means an acceleration of population ageing, not a slowdown. That means even bigger problems for our children. The rapidly growing immigration of people from Asian and African countries is likely to slow down population ageing, but in the future this could mean the same situation as in Finland and Sweden today. This means a very high rate of non-participation of one part of the population in the employment process, which in turn points to an increase in social problems. The restrictions provided for in this draft would apply only to so-called third-country nationals. They would not extend to citizens of the Member States of the European Union and the European Economic Area, the Swiss Confederation and the Organisation for Economic Cooperation and Development, as well as to doctoral students and foreign students who study in Estonian. Exceptions to labour migration would also remain. Raising wage levels is necessary for us to limit the introduction of low-cost labour into Estonia and the suppression of wage levels. The regulation of the entry of foreign students into Estonia is still necessary to ensure that we do not have other goals under the study sign. Now, according to a survey of the values of the Institute of Social Studies, only 23% of Estonian citizens and persons from other cultural spaces want large-scale immigration from the same cultural space. What is currently happening in Estonia is, in fact, large-scale immigration, which will make our well-being worse in the future or in the near future. The draft legislation on immigration prepared by the previous government had reached Parliament, but the government in power removed them from the procedure. But that doesn't mean the need for them is gone. And I'd say it's a keen need. Now I'm gonna finish. Ladies and gentlemen, if this bill needs to be amended, it must be discussed. But I'm sure you will understand that a change in immigration policy is absolutely necessary. I ask you to support this bill. Thank you for your attention.

ParlaMint-EE_2021-06-02_U354	Today's eighth item: Draft Act amending § 13 of the Citizenship Act, initiated by the Social Democratic Party Group and Riigikogu member Raimond Kaljulaid, 329. The rapporteur is Jevgeni Ossinovski, member of the Riigikogu. Please!
ParlaMint-EE_2021-06-02_U355	Mr President, I would like to thank you very much. Well, where are you all going now, such an important law! Mr Hepner, for example, is very happy to listen to important things; he has missed it. [[In the diner.]] Thank you. I present to you the draft law amending § 13 of the Citizenship Act, initiated by the Social Democratic Party Group 329. The draft consists of one sentence. In particular, it repeals section 13, paragraph 42 of the Citizenship Act and implements it retroactively from 17 February 2020. But of that, I think, you do not understand anything, and therefore I will explain to you the point of this draft and, on the basis of the debate in the Committee on Constitutional Affairs, a bit of a broader background. Having been in Parliament for ten years, I have discovered with surprise that so many young colleagues have been added who do not remember or have never known the various subcurrents of changes to the Citizenship Act. The aim of the draft is to correct one ugly provision that was put into the noble change made during the previous government. In fact, by the provision that came into force in February 17, 2020, we have allowed children born in Estonia and whose parent or grandparent has been in Estonia at the time of Estonia's regaining independence, to have their own person, to receive Estonian citizenship without examination, in a simplified way, if one parent is a person of undefined citizenship with a grey passport and the other parent is a third-country national, mostly 90% then a citizen of the Russian Federation. They will be able to obtain Estonian citizenship in a simplified way, and this is because we can move in that direction so that we stop producing Estonian citizenship again in the Russian Federation. The year was 2014, when the government of the Social Democrats and Reform Party, on my proposal, reached this agreement and also an amendment to the law with which we stopped producing grey passports in Estonia. So those children who are born in such a way that they can have undefined citizenship, because both their parents have a grey passport, will automatically receive Estonian citizenship if the parent does not "actively give up." With this, we had to stop producing a grey passport. This Decision shall apply from 1 January 2016. And in one year, about 800 children from 1,000 children who grew up without any nationality became Estonian citizens. In one year! This is also the last real change in Estonian citizenship policy, and its aim really is that all children born and raised in Estonia would have a relationship with the Estonian state based on Estonian citizenship. From there the matter has been discussed and proposals have been made on how to take the next step. Because, in addition to the re-production of the grey passport, we actually have the re-production of the citizenship of the Russian Federation, and this is actually happening at an increasing rate. Colleagues may not know that if one parent lives in Estonia with a grey passport and another with a Russian Federation passport, their child is automatically a citizen of the Russian Federation. It's automatically, even if he doesn't go to the Russian embassy, he won't take out his passport. For the Estonian state, it is a citizen of the Russian Federation who is born. Consequently, this amendment had been introduced. This was, in fact, already in the previous coalition, of which my party was also a party. It was proposed to allow these children to acquire Estonian citizenship in this way without examination. Then they would already grow as Estonian citizens. They're our kids, aren't they? At that time, this proposal was not realised as a result of differences of opinion within the coalition. In 2019 this much smaller piece of the proposal was realised and then entered into force on 17 February 2020. And if there was a solution in the then proposal that even if the child is a child of two Russian citizens, but he was born in Estonia, he could become an Estonian citizen without examination, then the compromise option was accepted that one parent must have a grey passport and the other one must be a third-country national. Then the child becomes an Estonian citizen without examination. I supported this amendment because it is also a step forward less than I would have liked, but the right step in the right direction. But what was written in it? This will now open the next complex legal procedure. In accordance with this amendment, which has entered into force, it is required that, in order for children to whom this right is essentially granted by law, must first give up the citizenship of the Russian Federation, prove to the Estonian state that they are no longer citizens of the Russian Federation. There is nothing to argue about the principle here, because there is a double citizenship ban in Estonia and one could say that at first glance it makes sense, makes sense. But here, once again, nuances need to be known. The nuance is that the Russian Federation does not exempt minors from Russian citizenship from its citizens. And this means in practice that this change, which entered into force last February and according to which, in theory, you could obtain Estonian citizenship without exams, will not help the 1400 children. In fact, 1300 of these children are those who cannot obtain Estonian citizenship in practice. Perhaps they will get confirmation from the Estonian state that they will be admitted to Estonian citizenship at the moment they submit an application and proof that they have been exempt from Russian citizenship. If the parents of this child go to the Russian Consulate and say that please release my child from the citizenship of the Russian Federation because he wants Estonian citizenship, then the Consul of the Russian Federation says that, unfortunately, this application cannot be accepted, because under the citizenship law of the Russian Federation minors will not be released from it. If you want to read more about this problem, you can write "The Case of the Kohtla-Järve Girl," which I dealt with in 2013, if I remember correctly. Then we had exactly the same problem: the Estonian state wanted to take Estonian citizenship away from the Estonian child because it could not give up the citizenship of the Russian Federation. This bill is based on the fact that the state should not impose conditions on its own citizen that it cannot fulfil for reasons beyond its control. The person wants to renounce the citizenship of the Russian Federation, but he cannot, the country will not allow him to leave its nationality. The intention of the draft is to waive this unreasonable and demeaning requirement and really give all those who have already, in essence, been granted the right to citizenship by the decision that came into force in February last year, even the possibility of obtaining Estonian citizenship. It is not necessary to require them, as a minor, to renounce the citizenship of the Russian Federation. Then we would really be able to have 1400 children, not 130, as has now come true in practice. Today, this entire target group has been around 1,400 children. During the debate in the Committee on Constitutional Affairs, Mrs Koidu Bee from the Ministry of the Interior reported that as of 18 May 59 of these 1400 children or 1500 children had submitted this application and that 10 children have actually obtained Estonian citizenship. So the major breakthrough that entered into force on 17 February last year has given Estonian citizenship to 10 people today. Well, there are some more children coming, because there are also some Ukrainian citizens among their parents, some Lithuanian citizens and one Portuguese citizen. They can theoretically give up, so maybe 10 becomes 15, maybe 20, but you understand the order of magnitude: basically, 1,500 children are granted citizenship, and today they're under 100. The purpose of our draft is that all 1500, on whom Parliament has made a fundamental decision, could indeed become an Estonian citizen without an examination, so that they can actually exercise this right. The biggest objection, which we will certainly hear today, concerns double citizenship. There is a ban on multi-citizenship in Estonia, and this change will lead us to give these children, who already have the majority of the citizenship of the Russian Federation, to Estonian citizenship and then have two passports: Estonian passport and Russian passport. Russian passport may not even exist because they may not have taken it out of the consulate, but let us say that they are considered to be citizens of the Russian Federation after birth. They have the opportunity to apply for this passport. And it is claimed that this could be contrary to the principles of Estonian citizenship policy. It is important to explain here that, according to these changes since 2016, to which I have already pointed out, the general rule of the Estonian Citizenship Act is that multiple citizenship is permitted for minors. For all minors in Estonia, double or multiple citizenship is permitted as a general rule, according to my memory in § 3. And this system works very easily, generally in the same way for everyone. You may have two passports, whether obtained by birth or later, up to the age of 18, but after that, a person must choose whether he or she wants to retain Estonian citizenship within three years and then he or she will really renounce the citizenship of the Russian Federation or he or she wishes to retain the citizenship of the Russian Federation and renounce Estonian citizenship or it will be taken away from him or her. Our proposal is to treat this particular group, the 1500 children who have been granted the right to Estonian citizenship, equally with all other minors within the same general rule. Yes, that means that these minors, these children, have a theoretical opportunity to receive a Russian Federation passport in addition to the Estonian passport, but this is not usually the case in practice. They are simply considered to be born-to-be citizens without documents on it. But then, when a person turns 18, the Ministry of the Interior really turns to them and says that now you have to decide and give up one or another citizenship in three years. Why do we think this is a better solution? Various integration studies confirm that children who have grown up as citizens have a stronger identity. If you have already grown as an Estonian citizen, there is a greater likelihood that at that moment, if you have to choose, you will actually opt for Estonian citizenship. You have grown in this country as a citizen of that country, and as a result you have a stronger connection with this country and society. And we do not really see any reason, indeed, no reason! just why this particular group of minors should be subject to more stringent rules on the exclusion of double citizenship than all other minors. There are really no substantive reasons for this. The reasons sought are, and they were also brought to the Commission's discussions. That these unfortunate children are a security threat to Estonia and all the other common talk that we hear during discussions on citizenship policy in the Republic of Estonia. We are talking about children born in Estonia, whose ancestors have lived here for generations. The fact that they receive Estonian citizenship, in addition to the citizenship of the Russian Federation, is a security threat! This is a broader question about the achievements and specificities of Estonian citizenship policy. I really believe that there are different opinions in the legitimate sense here, it must be acknowledged that one of the consequences of Estonian citizenship policy is that there are almost 100000 citizens of the Russian Federation in Estonia as permanent residents, not the success of Estonian citizenship policy, but the failure. I believe that the permanent residents of Estonia who live in this country could have a relationship with the Estonian state based on Estonian citizenship, and we could do anything to increase the share of Estonian citizens in Estonia. As far as adults are concerned, those people who were caught by the change in the regime in 1991 and then made their decision whether to stay with a grey passport or to take the Russian Federation's passport, it is a matter of its own, without doubt, also a politically highly emotionally charged issue. This bill does not deal with this. We're talking about kids right now. We are talking about children who have lived in Estonia, who have grown in Estonia, go to school here, and about the gesture of the Estonian state, that we actually recognise them as our children, as our own, and give them the citizenship of the Republic of Estonia, without requiring them to take steps that are simply legally impossible for them. In the debate that took place in the Committee on Constitutional Affairs, we heard, of course, these pragmatic arguments about the fundamentals of citizenship policy, these were all the stories that usually come to kill any substantive debate. However, as has been said, the principle of granting Estonian citizenship to this particular target group has been decided by the state, it has been legalised, it is already a rule in the Citizenship Act. The question is whether we actually allow these children to exercise this right, to actually exercise it, or whether we do not. Undoubtedly, those people who have little contact with the Russian-speaking population regard them as predominantly objects in Estonian society and are likely to find it difficult for them to know all these circumstances, including that it actually causes defiance. I have been approached by a number of parents who said when this law was passed that, well, I am so glad that in the end I have the opportunity to go and let my child know that I want Estonian citizenship. Well, I myself, as I am, have not learned this language, I am sorry for myself, but I do, but at least I want my child to be a citizen of this country and to start his life as a full-fledged citizen of Estonia. So it's really hard to explain that yes, it is, but, you know, you don't really have that right. Yes, you are submitting an application, yes, the Government of the Republic even adopts an order that you are an Estonian citizen, but it is beautifully written that this order that you become a citizen will enter into force only after you provide proof that you are exempt from the citizenship of the Russian Federation. This proof cannot be provided by this family for reasons beyond its control. I think it's all ugly. If a decision has been made that, in principle, these children have been granted the right to citizenship, then it should also be applied with dignity, so that these people can actually have that right. Of course, this is not the first time we have tabled this proposal. We also proposed this when this bill was being dealt with. At that time the coalition included the Centre Party, the Fatherland, and the EKRE. And then a politician of the Fatherland said that their goal was to give it to as few people as possible. Okay, you might realize that maybe it was ideologically meant to haunt the Centre Party: it's like you're getting some sort of election promise fulfilled, but really people can't exercise that right. Now the government has changed, and I think that the circumstances that were politically important at the time are in a slightly different light today. For example, representatives of the Centre Party have now supported the procedure for this draft in the new situation, and I think it is important to actually correct this ugliness born as a result of a certain political compromise. With our draft, we will also give this opportunity to Parliament. Let us make the arrangements for this change implemented last year so that these 1500 children can actually become Estonian citizens. Thank you!

ParlaMint-EE_2021-06-02_U357	Thank you for a very thorough presentation, honourable waitress. My question is whether this problem concerns only citizens of the Russian Federation or whether there are concerns with citizens of other countries as well. Do we only regenerate Russian citizens?
ParlaMint-EE_2021-06-02_U358	No, not just that. Naturally, in Estonia, children are born into mixed families, where the parents have a very diverse citizenship. I do not have a full review here, but in the context of this same change, Mrs Dawn Bee pointed out that the children who have taken advantage of this opportunity had a total of 59 out of 1500. Ten of them have acquired citizenship and the remaining 49 include 37 citizens of the Russian Federation, ten citizens of Ukraine, one Lithuanian national and one Portuguese national. The reason why I spoke overwhelmingly about the citizens of the Russian Federation is that it is indeed the largest group. More than 90% of those who are children who are third-country nationals are citizens of the Russian Federation. The rest of them are likely to be added by individual means, including from exotic countries, but it really does concern the citizens of the Russian Federation. That's one. Secondly, the question is that, in the case of the Russian Federation, we know exactly that there is such a practice. It is a long-standing practice, it is their choice of citizenship policy that they do not exempt their children from citizenship unless the parent and the child join the citizenship of another country together. They have their own logic. As far as I can see by reading their laws, they try to avoid children's citizenship. They let the child go with the parent to another country's nationality. For example, if an older citizen of the Russian Federation and his child want to leave the citizenship of the Russian Federation and enter Estonian citizenship, this is possible. But if the parent doesn't want and wants only the child, they won't. Only allowed with the parent. This is their choice of citizenship policy. There have certainly also been arguments here as to why they have such rules. I don't know why they have these rules. The Riigikogu of the Republic of Estonia does not have the opportunity to influence the citizenship policy of the Russian Federation, but we can influence our own in order to meet our people. A large number of countries, of course, allow the admission of Estonian citizenship in this context. But those children who belong to the citizenship of such countries are beautifully few in Estonia. Most of them belong to the citizenship of the Russian Federation.

ParlaMint-EE_2021-06-02_U364	Firstly, with regard to this particular case, which was the case of the Kohtla-Järve girl, it turned out that it is indeed the case that the Estonian state also considers a child who has one of the parents as a citizen of the Russian Federation to be a citizen of the Russian Federation after birth, even if there was no Russian passport requested and had nothing to do with the consulate. According to another section of the Citizenship Act, if the parent applies for Estonian citizenship, he or she can apply for citizenship to his or her child at the same time. Indeed, this possibility does not exist at present in practice.

That was about the thought I had here, too, and in the debate that took place in the Commission, I was reminded of the words of Bogoslovski, because it was extremely pleasant and, at the same time, completely useless. We saw the pressure being put on the same idea all the time. I didn't make up my first question in Blackwell's words in vain when I pointed out that there was nothing going on in politics without pressure. Indeed, it does not happen, only if there is pressure, and this pressure must be constant. But the wish has still been one, as I have already said: probably only quantity, quality is not important. As Evgeny has already pointed out here, you see, it is nice that five-year-old children want to have Estonian citizenship. I thought it was interesting why five-year-old children want Estonian citizenship. We all know that children, whether they are Russian citizens, the citizens of Burundi, the children of Finnish citizens who are here, are five years old, six years old or ten years old, have the same rights as the children of our citizens. So where does this legal pressure come from now? We know that the socialists have achieved that we have children who choose. Perhaps in the future, even younger children will choose, perhaps we will indeed see in the future how five - year - old children will choose. Well, then the pressure might come, and maybe that's interesting and exciting. Power and power and good luck to us! But, ladies and gentlemen, I'm sorry, I can't be very emotional. It is my task here to present to you what took place in the Commission, and the debate in the Commission, as I said, was exciting. On several days, we tried to shape our positions and make procedural decisions that we would present to you in this Chamber. And I'll introduce them. As Evgeny said, we have two lines in this amendment or proposal. We're talking about four or five words. § 2 of the draft act is an implementing provision concerning the date, and § 1 is the one where one point is amended. Indeed, there were two Commission meetings where we discussed them and took our decisions. The first was on 17 May this year, attended by Kalle Grünthal, Andrei Korobeinik, Oudekki Loone, Jevgeni Ossinovski, who was an alternate for Lauri Läänemets, myself, Paul Puustusmaa, Urmas Reinsalu, Marko Storm and Mart Võrguship. And just as Evgeny spoke very thoroughly and very enthusiastically about why this change was needed. He introduced the draft amendment to § 13 of the Citizenship Act to the Commission, explained that it concerns the amendments to the Citizenship Act that came into force in February 2020 About the application provision, if anyone asks why 17 February 2020 Evgeny stressed that also here. That's what Evgen said in the committee. This is a quote, it must not be mistaken: "The purpose of the previous government in making the amendment which entered into force in February 2020 was to make the circle of the beneficiaries of citizenship smaller and the explanatory memorandum to its draft (58 SE) stated that as of 12.08.2019 the draft deal concerned 1523 people who met the above-mentioned conditions for obtaining Estonian citizenship, 1393 of whom were citizens of the Russian Federation. " Evgeni also pointed out this controversial amendment to the Citizenship Act of the Russian Federation, or an apology, not an amendment, but Paragraph 19 (3) of the Citizenship Act of the Russian Federation, by which he justified this need. As we saw in this hall, it's not quite clear, and it really seems that Evgeny was trying to pull a little bag on us. But it's okay. Then Evgeni noted that as a result of the changes made by the previous government, there are quite a number of 'awaiting' Estonian citizens who cannot exercise their right to citizenship. There is nothing they can do to change this situation. According to Evgen, this was a politically vile step: it is a word-writing and it gives people the impression that they can become an Estonian citizen, but in fact they cannot become an Estonian citizen. Well, about such a summary. He then explained that the present draft solves this problem by repealing Paragraph 13 (42) of the Citizenship Act, which states that a minor wishing to be a citizen must provide proof of the surrender of citizenship of another country. If this provision were repealed, this means that those minors to whom the Estonian state has already granted the right to become a simplified Estonian citizen would actually become Estonian citizens and would no longer need to provide any evidence of the abandonment of citizenship of another state. He pointed out that there would probably be a question of how to solve the double citizenship problem in this draft, which he stressed here today as well. § 3 (1) of the Citizenship Act states that Estonia generally has a ban on double citizenship and if a person has received several citizenships as a minor, he or she must give up the citizenship of either Estonia or another state within three years of becoming 18 years of age. According to the draft, possession of several citizenships is possible until the age of age. Key moment! The representative of the Ministry of the Interior, Ruth Annus, also spoke at the Commission. That was his argument. Ruth Annus said that each country has a sovereign right to design its own citizenship policy and states do not interfere with each other's citizenship policy. The sovereign right to design its citizenship policy also means that Estonia cannot make its citizenship policy dependent on the citizenship policy of another country. The Ministry of the Interior is of the opinion that a person must not be double-citizen. If another country frees a person from his or her citizenship, he or she will be able to obtain Estonian citizenship. Ruth Annus also noted that in Estonia minors can acquire double citizenship by birth. The parents are usually of different nationality, one of the parents is an Estonian citizen. It is also possible to obtain double citizenship in such a way that one of the parents of a minor applies for Estonian citizenship under general conditions. To do so, the parent must meet certain requirements, pass an examination of Estonian language at least at B1, pass an examination of knowledge of the Constitution and Citizenship Act, have lived in Estonia for at least eight years and, among other things, take an oath to be loyal to the Republic of Estonia. The person can then also apply for Estonian citizenship for his/her child under 15 years of age. In such a case, this parent has a duty of loyalty to Estonia and it is justified that his or her child could have double citizenship until he or she reaches the age of adulthood. Ruth Annus said that Estonia has consistently not promised double citizenship because citizenship requires loyalty to its country. Estonian citizens have a duty of loyalty and a duty of national defence as compared to non-citizens, which results from the Constitution. Citizenship assumes that a person generally shares the basic values of the country of nationality as well as the political starting points. If the countries of nationality have significantly different fundamental values, a person will inevitably exercise the rights of a national of one country from the point of view of the fundamental values of another country. Man can only have one view of the world, not a different view of the world for each country. This is also one reason why Estonia has not consistently promised double citizenship. Ruth Annus also pointed out the fundamental constitutional problem that there is a ban on the withdrawal of Estonian citizenship from birth citizens in the Constitution and therefore there are persons in Estonia who have double citizenship. They do not behave legally, but they can continue with double citizenship, because the Constitution does not allow the citizenship acquired by birth to be taken away. At the same time, the Citizenship Act clearly states that an Estonian citizen cannot be at the same time as the citizenship of another state. He also pointed out the exception set out in the current law. The reference is then § 13 41 of the Citizenship Act, which allows minors to acquire Estonian citizenship and does not require the minor to fulfil any conditions other than the fact that the other state will exempt them from citizenship. These minors don't have to pass any exams. It is sufficient that one parent applies for citizenship for his or her child. The decision on the acquisition of nationality shall take effect when the minor has been released from his or her nationality by another State. I said that citizenship policy is based on two principles: one is ius sanguini, i.e. the acquisition of citizenship by parents, and the other is ius soli, i.e. citizenship is acquired by place of birth. These international principles have also been taken as a basis in Estonia. Estonian citizenship policy is based on the first, or bloodright, acquired citizenship. At the same time, Estonia has linked its citizenship policy to other principles, i.e. the principle of citizenship acquired on the basis of the right of birth, and this has caused many misunderstandings and confusion. In my opinion, Estonia could rather be more specific in its citizenship policy, and the exception that allows a person to have two citizenships is not right. In this respect, the Committee on Constitutional Affairs could consider adjusting the Constitution, as citizenship policy could be clear, concrete and understandable. I noted that, as far as draft 329 is concerned, the Group of the Estonian Conservative People's Party is against it. Then I asked the Dose why the Government of the Republic gave up its opinion on Bill 309. Ruth Annus replied that giving up the Government of the Republic's opinion was a political decision. Jevgeni Ossinovski, for his part, stated that, as far as he knew, the Minister of the Interior had sent a letter to the State Chancellery on 10 March, which included a proposal by the Minister of the Interior not to form a position on the draft 309 to the Government of the Republic. The Government of the Republic therefore followed the proposal of the Minister of the Interior. Jevgeni Ossinovski also found that the interest of the Estonian state could be that children living in Estonia would grow as Estonian citizens, and it does not matter what their children's parents want for their children. Estonia's long-term interest could be that all people living permanently in Estonia would have a relationship with Estonia based on citizenship. Jevgeni Ossinovski also noted that a wider list of cases where a person does not have to give up the citizenship of another country in order to gain Estonian citizenship was offered in his time. One option was that if, for reasons beyond the control of the person, he was unable to give up the citizenship of another country, so that the Estonian state would not require it. This change, of course, did not find any support in its time. Urmas Reinsalu said that Jevgeni Ossinovski indicated that the number of minors who could benefit from simplified citizenship under the current Citizenship Act was about 130 and asked whether the number referred to was correct according to the Ministry of the Interior. Ruth Annus replied that, as of 12 August 2019, the change made by the previous government affected about 1,500 people, and there were just under 1,400 people left, who were then citizens of the Russian Federation. That was also the factual fact that was referred to here. Urmas Reinsalu also noted that this change in the previous government has been in force for a little over a year and asked how many people have exercised the right granted to them during this period or how much Estonian citizenship has thereby gained. Dawn Bee replied that, as of today, 59 people have applied for citizenship under this provision and ten of them have received citizenship. For the rest, the granting of citizenship has not yet entered into force, as children are still nationals of another country. Among the 49 children against whom the decision to grant citizenship has not entered into force, 37 citizens of the Russian Federation, ten citizens of Ukraine, one Lithuanian national, one Portuguese national. Andrei Korobeinik found that he considered the bill 309 to be a very good draft, because the Estonian state should not be strict in granting its citizenship. There could be a little more empathy in all of this. Toomas Kivimägi found that the general principle of Estonia is that double citizenship is not allowed and, if this general principle is changed, there must be very compelling arguments and this change should concern a considerable number of people. Kivimägi asked what was the reason for initiating this draft of the Social Democratic Party Group: is this the result of the enormous desire of people who could potentially gain citizenship as a result of this change? Kivimägi noted that it would not be reasonable to change the general principle of Estonian citizenship policy if a few dozen people wished to change, and asked how much interest there could be in such a change. It is now clear that those minors who submitted the application will have the right to Estonian citizenship when they are 18 years old. Jevgeni Ossinovski replied that two governments back in the coalition had an agreement that children, both of whose parents are citizens of the Russian Federation, could become Estonian citizens, and there were about 8000 of them at the time. This amendment was not made. At the time, the idea of a change had been proposed to stop the re-production of Russian Federation citizenship in Estonia. The fact that there are 100000 citizens of the Russian Federation in Estonia is not good for Estonia. Estonia could want us to have more Estonian citizens. The representative of the Ministry of Internal Affairs also noted that, in addition to everything else, there is no way to underestimate the security threat arising from double citizenship of Estonia and Russia. Oudekki Loone said that Estonia should give a clear signal that Estonia wants all good people in Estonia to be recognised as members of its political community. Mr Reinsalu pointed out that he did not support this draft. It is neither practical nor reasonable to extend such an extensive citizenship policy, which would be created by the possibility of double citizenship. Jevgeni Ossinovski stated at the end that it is true that Estonia has an impact on citizenship policy due to a certain historical reality, which is reflected in decisions made in citizenship policy, but it is not so important. In a long view, Estonia should want those who are born and growing in Estonia to be Estonian citizens, and this is the purpose of the bill. Ruth Annus, representative of the Ministry of the Interior, said in her last word that the final stage was either a political decision to support the draft or not. The Ministry of the Interior considers that it is not reasonable to underestimate the security threat posed by the double citizenship of Estonia and Russia. One previous coalition agreement spoke of 8,000 young Estonian-Russian double citizens and the Ministry of the Interior considers this to be a serious security threat. Ruth Annus also noted that there is a significant difference, because persons with citizenship have political rights to shape the Estonian legal space, and non-citizens do not have these rights. However, social guarantees are indeed the same for all. Toomas Kivimägi proposed to continue the debate in the Commission on 18 May and after that the procedural decisions that were finally adopted were as follows. First, it was proposed that the draft be placed on the agenda of the plenary session on 2 June; this decision was adopted by consensus. The second proposal was to reject the draft. There were 5 people in favour of rejection: Toomas Kivimägi, Paul Puustusmaa, Urmas Reinsalu, Marko Storm, Mart Võrklaev, against 3: Andrei Korobeinik, Oudekki Loone, Lauri Läänemets, there were no neutrals. The third decision was to appoint a member of the Committee on Constitutional Affairs, Paul Puustusmaa, i.e. I, that decision was consensual. Thank you very much.
