ParlaMint-EE_2021-06-02_U469	Our ninth item on the agenda today is the development plan for the Estonian language for the years 2021 to 2035. Before we go to this procedure, I will remind you how we deal with it. First of all, the Minister for Education and Science, Liina Kersna, will receive the floor for a presentation of up to 20 minutes. Then Signe Kivil, a member of the Committee on Culture, will have a presentation for up to 20 minutes. A member of the Riigikogu may ask an oral question to each of the two rapporteurs. Please, Minister of Education and Science Liina Kersna!
ParlaMint-EE_2021-06-02_U470	Mr President, I would like to thank you very much. Happy members of the Riigikogu and everyone else in the hall! I am very pleased to present to you the Estonian language development plan, which sets out the goals and trends of the Estonian language field for the years 2021 to 2035. The development plan has been developed in cooperation between the Ministry of Education and Research and the Estonian Language Council, and many institutions and experts involved in the language field have contributed to its completion. The development plan for the language area is a plan combining ministries and institutions in terms of objectives and activities, which we manage from the Ministry of Education and Research, but others contribute to its implementation. The proliferation of language speeches, debates and opinions that have recently been held in public provides evidence that the language landscape does not leave anyone indifferent and requires further attention and intervention. However, what to consider, why and how to intervene, we provide answers to this in the language development plan. Although the language field may seem to be quite abstract and spontaneous, it is specific and systematic enough. In the past, language activities were more related to education, philology, literature and communication, in particular by performing the functions of a communication tool. Today, the language sector has other links, such as entrepreneurship, information technology, law, medicine and data science. The development of technology has significantly broadened the boundaries of the language field. According to the Ethnology database, Estonian is one of 50 languages with higher education and science in their own language. Estonian is one of 100 languages with language technology support and one of up to 30 languages with effective speech synthesis. Thanks to the fact that Estonian is among the 24 official languages of the European Union, the modern Estonian language has been developed with new terms. Studies in linguistics and language status provide sufficient proof that Estonian is sustainable and has a high status. You can safely claim that Estonian is one of the most advanced cultural languages and in this sense is ranked among the best 1% in the world. In a globalizing world, however, this knowledge gives us no reason to give in to our efforts. On the contrary, we need to address the three cornerstones of language development and sustainability even more systematically and effectively. These are reputation and status, language infrastructure and language learning. In this context, the general objective of the Estonian language development plan is worded as follows: "To ensure the vitality and functioning of the Estonian language as a primary language in all areas of life, to guarantee everyone the right and opportunity to use the Estonian language in the Republic of Estonia, to maintain and strengthen the status, reputation and cultural space of the Estonian language and to value the ability to speak other languages." The substantive structure of the development plan is divided into three parts. Firstly, language status and reputation, or attitudes towards language, and the legal position of the language. Secondly, language infrastructure, or our language park, with dictionaries, corps, linguistics and language technology. And third, of course, language learning and its supporting activities. As the development plan has set goals for development, I will focus on the main problems in the language field and how to solve them. The reputation and status of the Estonian language guide people's linguistic choices. Although Estonian is a world-class cultural language, Estonians value it as a bearer of national identity, the state of the Estonian language is not without problems. Youth language attitudes, especially in the common media and digital environments, tend towards other languages, particularly English. When we acquire Estonian dialectal languages, the intergenerational bond is breaking, although in the public space we meet dialectal languages before, see more. There are areas where the use of the Estonian language has decreased, and there have been additional jobs in the labour market, where the requirements for the proficiency of the Estonian language have not been regulated. The overall loss of linguistic diversity and changes in the labour market may weaken the use of the Estonian language. Among the other native- or native-language population, Estonian language skills have not improved significantly in the last ten years. In the development plan for the language area, we have set the goal of increasing the status of the Estonian language and strengthening its reputation. To this end, the use, study and study of Estonian should be popularized and encouraged. The importance of the Estonian language as a bearer of identity and the timeliness of the legislation in the field of language must be monitored. The development plan includes activities that encourage the use of Estonian language, including dialect languages, and popularize language learning. Support will be given to activities that contribute more to the principles of a clear language, including systematic and science-based language care. The monitoring of compliance with the language law will be enhanced in such a way as to support and not discourage language learning. Language infrastructure and language technology support language use. We have already achieved quite a lot in the development of language infrastructure and language technology. All language resources, language resources and language technology tools created by the state are available free of charge to the users of Estonian language. Top-level language technology support already allows Estonian to be used in several digital environments. If we are already state-of-the-art, what are the following trends? It is easy to say that if the computer was previously dealt with how to teach Estonian, then it is now dealt with how the computer would learn the Estonian language better. In order to encourage this self-study, a language corps that is as large and of high quality as possible is needed. Language bottlenecks are the uneven quality of the corps or even the lack of them. In international comparison, important components of language technology, such as the control of grammar in Estonian, are missing or still in development. Estonia also needs effective coordination of term work, as the layer of the vocabulary changes as quickly as the respective specialties. Since language is constantly changing, it is clear that we cannot allow any stagnation in the development of language infrastructure. In the development plan for the language area, we have set a goal: the study of Estonian is internationally high and the language infrastructure is innovative, open and diverse, and the skills in Estonian are developed in a coordinated manner. To this end, investment in language infrastructure must be increased and the development of Estonian language science as a direction for research and development in the language field must be supported. We will create better opportunities to develop language infrastructure in cooperation with businesses, higher education institutions and research institutions. We will enhance the term work so that the new terms can be quickly recycled and the Estonian language can be easily used in all disciplines. We take Estonian language technology to all users in both digital environments and smart devices. We support the development and learning of Estonian sign language, including using language technology tools. Third, language training. The subjects of language learning are divided between two sectoral development plans: language and education. This means that achieving the objectives set in language learning requires interdisciplinary cooperation. Part of the education sector is the organisation of the education system, the volume of studies and the follow-up of teachers. The framework of the Estonian language development plan is based on the creation of language support tools, improving the language skills of educators, supporting the professional development of language teachers and integrating language technology into language learning. Thematically, language learning activities cover three major topics in the language development plan: Estonian as a native language, Estonian as a second language and multilingualism. There are a number of problems to be solved here, but I would like to mention the most important ones. Trends in the globalising world also put pressure on the Estonian language. We must therefore pay attention to the high level of proficiency in the Estonian language of native learners. It is not in line with society's expectations how the language of education in Estonian schools has been solved so far. The Estonian language skills of Estonian residents must be sufficient to enable them to acquire education and participate in the labour market without hindrance. Studies show that Estonian language skills have increased a little, but not enough, among the other native or native-language population in the last ten years. The need for adult language learning is very high, but language learning opportunities do not meet the needs of all learners. Estonia also fails to meet the European Union's recommended goal of knowing two other foreign languages in addition to its mother tongue. This means that nearly half of adults' knowledge of a foreign language is limited to one language. For a small country, the ability to communicate in other languages is a matter of strategic importance, which is why we want to complement the foreign language skills of Estonian residents. Assumptions have been made for this purpose, as Estonian residents are aware that it is useful to know at least two foreign languages in the context of the labour market. To grasp all these topics, we have set in the development plan of the language area the goal that all Estonians and Estonians living abroad can speak Estonian and value the skills of other languages. To this end, we plan the following activities: We enhance the teaching of Estonian language, support the development and implementation of the Estonian-language education development plan. We provide language technology support for the preparation of educational materials. In cooperation with higher education institutions, we develop principles for the language and internationalisation of higher education. We organise the teaching of the adult Estonian language as a second language and ensure that Estonians living abroad learn the Estonian language. To sum it up. Given the assumption that the economic and cultural capacity of Estonian society will increase, we want to achieve the following results in 2035. First of all, the reputation of the Estonian language is increasing among people with both Estonian native language and other native or native language, and the proportion of people using the Estonian language in the population has increased. Secondly, the main components of language technology have been developed at internationally comparable levels. Thirdly, the term work is effective and is centrally coordinated. Fourthly, adult education in Estonian as a second language is organised in the country as a whole and is based on the needs of the learner. Fifthly, the self-esteemed foreign language skills of Estonian residents have improved and foreign languages are studied in many different forms. In order to ensure the vitality and functioning of the Estonian language, we will carry out various development activities within the framework of the development plan in the next 15 years. We develop language infrastructure to keep language technology internationally competitive. We strengthen the image of the Estonian language and solve the challenges of multilingualism and the language of education. I very much hope that you will support the objectives and activities set out in the development plan for the language area. Thank you.

Professor Birute Klaas-Lang gave an overview of the background to the birth of the development plan. The previous Estonian language development plan was valid until 2017 and extended for three years. In 2019, language scientists convened two seminars by way of a citizens' initiative, attended by representatives of all language councils throughout the ages, the Ministry and also the State Chancellery. The main problems and objectives of the new development plan were defined. Birute Klaas-Lang stressed that the development plan was the result of the cooperation and inclusion process and considered it important that the legal space function in such a way that the sustainability of the Estonian language is guaranteed, which is also the task of the state. Birute Klaas also stressed that Estonian society is ready for a political decision on the subject of the Estonian-language school. A debate took place in which Viktoria Ladynskaja-Kubits, Eduard Odines, Aadu Black, Heidy Purga and Signe Kivi participated. Minister Ab explained that the educational development plan, the language development plan and the coherent population development plan all pursue the same objectives. Although the wordings of the development plans do not overlap by 100%, the content is aimed at teaching mostly in Estonian. It takes time and it doesn't happen overnight. ParlaMint-EE_2021-06-02_U512	Good chairman of the sitting! Ladies and gentlemen, In particular, I would like to thank all the authors of the Estonian language development plan and the contributors to this very thorough process, especially the Estonian Language Council. The work has been done seriously and thoroughly. Very important objectives have been set: the status and reputation of the Estonian language, studying the Estonian language, developing the language infrastructure, mastering the Estonian language and valuing the skills of other languages. The Social Democrats basically support the objectives of the development plan, but there are still some issues that I would like to draw colleagues' attention to. From the point of view of language research, I am concerned about the funding of research and research on this subject. In Estonia, where the preservation of the mother tongue and culture is considered to be a constitutional value, the funding of humanities is considerably lower than in other disciplines. The previous Minister of Culture initiated a programme of research and development in humanities, including cultural and linguistic fields, in order to enhance the humanities' ability to contribute to the development of the Estonian language and cultural space. The sole source of funding for the programme is the European Regional Development Fund (ERDF) of EUR 3 million for nine years. And it is paid in the framework of a smart specialisation measure, which means a significant departure from the original objective. From the point of view of the knowledge of Estonian language, the development plan states that Estonia will switch to education in Estonian, that the teaching in schools and kindergartens of the state and local governments will take place in Estonian. But the plan to move towards this goal remains unclear. There is a reference to the development plan for education. The Education Development Plan does not provide for such an objective. It is thus unclear how the objective of the language development plan will be achieved. It was good to find out from the minister that the aim of the language development plan would remain. At least to the Minister, this is clear. The transition to education in Estonian is as it is. Government departments have different stories, starting from the fact that there is no transition until the transition begins now. In this confusion of messages, it is difficult to believe that the objective set out in the language development plan can be realised at all. In addition, no metrics have been set in the development plan for the transition to education in Estonian, which once again makes it possible to question its thought-out and credibility. At this point, I would like to stress that, in order to achieve this goal, the social democrats feel that it is more necessary to develop the idea of a co-educating school and to achieve a situation that guarantees the integration of children and young people in other languages into Estonia's cultural and value area and gives them an equal opportunity to participate in social and professional life and to continue their studies. RITA migration under the project has developed different models. Let us seize them and bring them to life! As an important activity, the development plan states that the study of Estonian as a second language would be of high quality and accessible, and that the number of people who speak the Estonian language would significantly increase. A very reasonable goal indeed. However, looking back at the metrics, we can see that the yardstick for this goal is to create an environment for adult learners in the organisation of the teaching of Estonian as a second language. Once again, there is a very weak connection that causes conflicting feelings. In addition, it is known that most of the resources for organising adult Estonian language learning are in the area of the Ministry of Culture: the structural resources of the European Union, the funding of Estonian language houses from the state budget. The lack of coordination between the two ministries has been repeatedly referred to, including in the report of the National Audit Office. With concern, I also think that if the aim of mastering the Estonian language of all Estonian residents is not achieved for political or other reasons, whether it is possible to talk about achieving the high status and reputational purpose of the Estonian language. Hopefully, the Government of the Republic, which approves the development plan, will also have a plan B in place. At the same time, the results of integration monitoring show that the proficiency of the Estonian language in Estonian society is improving from year to year. So there is hope that I have nothing to worry about. But another contradiction that I would like to highlight in the language development plan. On the one hand, the aim of the development plan is that children with a different mother tongue from the Estonian language will receive their native language education, on the other hand, it is stipulated that the topic of the mother tongue of ethnic minorities will be dealt with by a different development plan, a coherent social development plan under the direction of the Ministry of Culture. Social Democrats have repeatedly raised the issue that Russian as a foreign language for Russian-speaking children in a school with an Estonian language of instruction is not correct. Russian language education should also be organised as a native language education in a school with an Estonian language of instruction. The Minister of Education and Science has promised to take this matter seriously. We look forward to the results of this serious challenge. And to conclude. Ladies and gentlemen, the objectives and actions are broadly correct, but, to a significant extent, they relate to other development plans, with indicators for achieving these objectives being largely in other development plans and strategies. This is a serious concern, as the objectives of the various development plans seem to conflict in some ways. At the same time, this development plan and, for example, the education development plan are prepared by the previous government and hopefully the new government will be able to resolve these contradictions. ParlaMint-EE_2021-06-02_U526	Happy Members of the Riigikogu! Dear Minister, I am pleased to speak on behalf of the Reform Party, which has always stood up for the protection and development of the Estonian language. The inclusion of the Estonian language in the Preamble of the Constitution of the Republic of Estonia in 2007 took place on the initiative of the Reform Party, when we supported the proposal of the Director of the Institute of Estonian Language at the time and the Estonian Language Council to supplement the Preamble of the Constitution of the Republic of Estonia, adding a language in addition to the protection of Estonian nationality and culture. On 21 July 2007, the current version of the Constitution of the Republic of Estonia entered into force, which states: "Unwavering in faith and unwavering will to consolidate and develop the state [---], which must ensure the preservation of the Estonian nation, language and culture throughout the ages. " The national curricula of primary school and upper secondary school adopted in 2011 follow the wording of the Constitution of the Republic of Estonia and also say clearly: Estonian school stands for the preservation and development of Estonian nation, language and culture. Therefore, in upper secondary school as well as in comprehensive school education and education, special attention is paid to the teaching of Estonian. Now for the development plan. Today we redeem our debt, debt to the Estonian Language Council, linguists and all the good partners who have been preparing the development plan for the Estonian language for years. It is indeed a shame that Estonia has not had an Estonian language development plan since 2017. The new Estonian language development plan sets Estonia's language policy goals and trends for the years 2021 to 2035 and says strongly about Estonian language in Estonian school: by 2035 it must be mostly transferred to Estonian-language school education, i.e. teaching in schools and kindergartens funded by the state and local authorities will take place in Estonian. This is a very important sign, an important target device. The development plan is about the status and reputation of the Estonian language, the study of the Estonian language and the infrastructure, as well as the learning of the Estonian language. Let me say a few words about everyone. The status and reputation of the Estonian language is, as linguists say, that language is a tool on the one hand and an identity on the other. Depends on how much we ourselves believe, how much the whole nation believes that being an Estonian is proud and good and that the Estonian language has a future, how much tears will bring to our eyes in Hando Runnel's poem: "One very old nation with the earth's wisdom in its mouth, / greeted us from time to time and said, we will be new,/and said, we will be alive, we will be still ahead of us. And when we heard that happiness, we had eyes in the water. " Why do we have eyes in the water? Why do we not believe that Estonian has a future? I remember writing my first articles of opinion in the 1980s when I got upset by Trivimi Velliste, the Aru Club for Heritage Protection, that we should not be told that we are a small nation. Researchers of language attitudes say that Estonians really underestimate their language and that inferiority is the fastest doomer. Some linguists believe that it is only a matter of time before we are ready to switch to another language. At the same time, we have no reason to be so-called inferior. Indeed, there are 50 languages in the world in which you can obtain higher education, Estonian among them. Windows and MS Office can only be used in 35 cultural languages. We can do that. We are the official language of the European Union, one of the 200 different national languages. Studying the Estonian language, developing the language infrastructure, developing the vocabulary in Estonian is essential for the Estonian language to survive. This is the second objective of the development plan. However, my continuing concern is whether we understand the importance of basic research. Indeed, language technology is a very important priority and I am pleased that the development plan for Estonian language sees language technology as a priority area. Indeed, it depends on how much we can use a wide range of technologies in the future. According to senior researcher Einar Meister at Tallinn University of Technology, the language will depend on the number of speakers, for example, whether private companies will make speech recognition. Language technology tools can be used successfully for business purposes for English and other languages with a large number of speakers. But according to Einar Meister, a dozen or more years ago, it was estimated that if there are less than 10 million speakers in the language, it does not make economic sense. This means that state support for the development of language technology is very important. It is very nice that the Estonian Ministry of Education and Research has been supporting language technology programmes since 2006. But the development of language technology also requires basic research, a broader study of the Estonian language. The development of language technology requires both data science and artificial intelligence. This is indeed a THAILAND issue that we adopted here a few weeks ago. However, how do we use structural funds, which, unfortunately, are only being narrowly directed towards smart specialisation and applied sciences? Knowledge of both language and computer science as well as cognitive psychology is also necessary for the processing of Estonian language. In order to automatically translate texts, it is necessary to start with the identification of bends and turns, the marking of sentence limits, and to stop collecting sophisticated training material for artificial neural networks. And thirdly, the teaching of Estonian. The development plan aims to ensure that all residents of Estonia can speak Estonian and value the skills of other languages. This is crucial. It is important for us to have equal opportunities for all residents to live a good life, to participate in working life, to integrate into society, but it is also a security issue. You could say that the last decision was made in the field of education in Estonia, namely language: the transition to study in Estonian at upper secondary school was precisely determined. That was probably not enough, for the current situation is still catastrophic. You could say that we have 300,000 people in the working age population whose native language is not Estonian. A survey of the Estonian language of adults, carried out in 2017 by CentAR, shows that only about half of the people living in Estonia with a different native language know the Estonian language well, or very well, according to their own estimates. 11% of our 15- to 74-year-olds, or 150,000, do not speak Estonian at all or are at a bad level, and several hundred young people graduate from primary school in Estonia each year, whose level of Estonian does not suffer criticism. It is therefore very important that the Ministry of Education and Research has decided to develop an action plan for the transition to education in Estonian. This means not only language learning, but also integration into language, custom and value space. It is great that the Estonian language development plan states that Estonian-language school education guarantees the integration of children and young people into the Estonian language and value area and gives everyone an equal opportunity to participate in social and professional life and continue their studies at the next level of education. In conclusion, I would say so. I am delighted that teachers, school leaders, parents, as well as the development of didactics and teaching tools are a priority in the action plan of the Ministry of Education and Research. Without developing these tools, it is impossible for us to reach the goal of moving to an Estonian-language school by 2035. Lots of strength for all of us! Thank you.

ParlaMint-EE_2021-06-02_U534	Let us move on to our 10th item on the agenda today. This is a presentation of the implementation of the Culture Policy Decision until 2020 and is made by Anneli Ott, Minister of Culture. Anneli Oti's report is up to 30 minutes. Please!
ParlaMint-EE_2021-06-02_U535	Mr President, I would like to thank you very much. Happy Members of the Riigikogu! I am pleased to present you with a report on the implementation of the fundamentals of cultural policy during the 2014-20 period. Strong traditions play an important role in Estonian culture, which we are very proud of. Culture is important and the result matters very much to the people of Estonia. Even if we do not show it every day, the love of cultural traditions is strongly expressed at the Song and Dance and at all when it comes to symbol values and keeping them. The cultural field is broad and interwoven with many other areas of life. Culture is like an iceberg: the tip can be visible and experienced, but below the waterline is a much wider sense of size, invisible, personal sense of each one of us. Culture has many meanings and layers, all of which we are carriers. Estonian cultural policy has been guided in the last seven years by the strategy paper Culture 2020 which was confirmed in the Riigikogu in 2014. The strategy agreed on the objectives and priorities for cultural policy. Today's presentation summarises a rather long period of time and we are already going on with a new strategic agreement, which is the "Cultural Development Plan 2021 to 2030," which I hope I will present to you before the end of the spring session. Seven years ago, we made cultural policy the goal of creating a society of value for creativity, preserving and promoting Estonian national identity, studying, storing and transferring cultural memory and creating favourable conditions for the development and participation in a vibrant, open and diverse cultural space. Due to the objectives set, we have taken a number of important steps in implementing Estonian cultural policy over the years, starting with a consistent increase in the minimum wage for cultural workers and cultural memory, creating favourable conditions for the development and participation in a vibrant, open and diverse cultural space. Due to the objectives set, we have taken a number of important steps in the process of advancing Estonian cultural policy over these years. Now some examples of important job victories contributed not only to the Ministry of Culture but also to the Riigikogu and, in fact, to society as a whole. First of all, from 2020, which presented a wide range of challenges to the cultural field and was clearly distinct throughout the period. The global pandemic had a significant impact on participation in cultural life, the possibilities for creating culture and the whole of Estonia's cultural sphere as a whole. The Ministry of Culture has tried to keep as many jobs and structures as possible in this crisis. Yet, we were forced to pause cultural life temporarily. The special situation in March last year closed the theatre halls, and the second half of 2020 was undoubtedly difficult for the performing institutions. Most of the major festivals were canceled, and many amateur and school theatre festivals either moved to the web or were canceled. In the current situation, the Ministry of Culture had to rapidly develop aid measures for the sector as a whole, also ensuring their rapid implementation. The country's recent 25 million crisis measures have supported the sustainability of cultural life, but the important question is what the crisis has left behind for creation and the motivation of creators, the spirit power and the joy of creation. We will not see these effects until next few years. Happy Members of the Riigikogu! When it comes to the development of cultural policy over the last six years, I will start with the pay of cultural workers, which has been on a growing trend throughout the period. In 2014, the aim was to increase the pay of cultural workers and bring it to the level of the average wage in Estonia. At that time, the national minimum wage for cultural workers was EUR 732, with an average salary of EUR 1005. The scissors between Estonia's average gross wage and the minimum wage for cultural workers with higher education were therefore more than 30%. In 2020, Estonia's average salary was 1448 euros and the minimum fee for a cultural worker with a higher education was at least 1300 euros. So we have reached about 10% of the gap. I would like to make it clear that the national wage agreement with the Central Organisation of Trade Unions for Employees concerns cultural workers who receive a salary from the Ministry of Culture's budget, but this signicant progress has undoubtedly helped to equalise the situation in other cultural institutions as well. The TALO has called for the salary agreement to be extended to all cultural workers, regardless of the administrative authority of their employers. In the field of culture, there is an important and broad-based target group with no specific employers and irregular incomes. We are talking about freelance artists, whose number increased by 1100 during the period: from 4505 to 5632, or almost a quarter. We have been working throughout the whole period to ensure social guarantees for freelance artists, in particular health insurance. A study is currently underway which, for the first time, identifies specific concerns about the economic situation and social guarantees of freelance artists. The role of creativity in society has increased and the practices and forms of employment have changed. By analysing the bottlenecks of the Creative People's and Creative Unions Act, I hope that we can agree on new solutions in cooperation with creative areas this year and translate them into a draft amending the Act. One of the pillars of the continuity of the Estonian state is the belief of people in our culture and its continuity. The survival of our culture depends primarily on people who pass on culture and tradition to the next generation. The diversity of opportunities offered in the cultural landscape depends on whether we pass on the culture we have enriched with elements or whether culture loses its value for future generations. The country is working towards increasing opportunities for people to participate in cultural life through support programmes, cultural events and initiatives. Since 2014, the state has supported the development of a regional cinema network through the Cultural Endowment of Estonia and the acquisition of digital projection and sound equipment in cinemas, resulting in modern cinema viewing in all Estonian counties. This resulted in a number of smaller places, such as Võru, Tõrva, Kilingi-Nõmme, Kose, Põltsamaa, Mustvee, Rapla, Järva-Jaan and Vändra's new projection techniques, which significantly improved the availability of cinema art in Estonia. It is also positive that there are many different museums in Estonia, a total of 227. When travelling around Estonia, museums can also be found in smaller villages and almost everywhere there is an opportunity to participate in attractive and educational exhibitions. The interest in museums is high and the visitation of museums has increased considerably over the last decade. There are visitors both from Estonia and from outside. The theatre and dance can be taken outside the attraction centers by the "Theatre in the Land" support programme. For example, in 2016, 46000 people from all over Estonia participated in theatre art. There is a lot of positive, but we must certainly work to ensure that the opportunities for participation in culture in Estonian towns and rural areas are not too different. There is room for improvement because, for example, as many as 417 of the 966 art exhibitions held in Estonia in 2019, or almost half of them took place in Tallinn. There is certainly a need to find a balance here, because statistics show that the people of Tartumaa and Tallinn and the rest of Harjumaa were the most able to participate in the active cultural life, with 83% of the inhabitants of Valgamaa being the least able to participate: 64%. Thus, both the state and local authorities should continue to contribute to the regional availability and balance of culture. The creation of a cultural pack facility, reflected in the government's coalition agreement, will certainly contribute to this, which will increase the chances for young people to participate in culture. Theatrical performances and museum visits, which are impressive, want to be experienced by all members of society. I therefore consider it important to address the issue of accessibility. It is certainly positive that more attention will be paid to this year after year. For example, in 2018 the Ministry of Culture ordered the Estonian National Broadcasting to supply six films with a subtitling translation in Estonian for hearing impaired. During 2018, both Endla and Ugala theatre gave performances in the field of performing arts. For the Estonian Blind Library, a small amount of 5,155 sound and e-books, newspapers and magazines in Estonian and foreign languages have been produced every year and made available to 960 readers in the online library. In the case of museums, good examples are the Estonian Maritime Museum and the Estonian Art Museum with their educational programmes, which can be used by children, young people and adults with mobility, vision, hearing, intelligence and composite disabilities. The amendment to the Museum Act, which allows free or more favourable access to museums in the country to a number of vulnerable groups of society, has also helped. The launch of the Accessibility Task Force set up by the Ministry of Culture in 2019 is also marked. The first proposals on how to develop public space and its services accessible to all people over the next ten years are expected soon. We are no longer talking only about the ramps ahead of the theatre, but we are seeing the problem much more widely and there is also the will to solve them. Good ambassadors! The tradition of song and dance touches us all. That's the natural part of being us without which you can't even imagine yourself. As a bearer of national identity, the party movement is also rightly included in the UNESCO list of masterpieces of oral and spiritual heritage. Two general singing and dance parties and one young singing and dance festivals took place during the period 2014-2020. At the last Song and Dance "My Love" held in 2019, we celebrated the 150th anniversary of the first general song festival in Tartu. Although the tradition of song and dance seems to be strong and sustainable, the continuation of parties in the form we know of cannot be taken for granted to the end. The problems of young tutors in the Song and Dance movement have been recognised for years. The latest study, presented in 2019, found that the invitation of the instructor of young singing and dance festivals is not motivating, as it is not sufficiently remunerated and does not provide adequate protection for unemployment and health insurance. More than half of current supervisors will soon retire and the collectives will also not be economically sustainable. The tradition of the Song and Dance Party must not be underestimated in order to preserve our nation, language and culture, and therefore this year the labour cost support measure was launched for the instructors of the collectives involved in the song and dance movement. It will amount to EUR 2.7 million. Hopefully, this will motivate both parents and younger tutors to contribute to the survival of the UNESCO-listed tradition. In the development of the field of folk culture, the priorities are maintaining and valuing the cultural heritage of the people, maintaining the tradition of singing and dance and creating the prerequisites for development through operating grants to central and important regional organisations in the culture of the people. The strength of Estonian folk culture is a national heritage, and people who value and carry local customs and lifestyles pass it on to future generations, thus keeping folk culture viable. To support Estonian heritage culture, the Ministry of Culture has developed seven cultural space support measures: Setomaa, Kihnu, islands, Peipsiveere, Mulgimaa, Vana-Võrmaa and Virumaa. The aim of these programmes is to preserve the identity of the different regions, their linguistic specificity and their diverse culture. People's culture needs constant attention and commitment. In 2020, 79753 enthusiasts participated in active folk culture groups. This is a major indicator, but there is concern about the falling trend, which has been further driven by the crisis. The problem is obvious, but hopefully the concept of a support system for youth interest activities, developed under the leadership of the Ministry of Culture and implemented in 2017, aimed at increasing the participation of children and young people aged 7 to 19 in regular activities of interest, improving its availability and quality and ensuring a more diverse selection in all areas of Estonia. One of the main events in the field of folk culture was the presence of Setomaa Obinitsa village in the Finno-Ugric Capital of Culture in 2015. Obinitsa, as a small community, was able to make a major and important effort by forcefully raising the focus of her village, community and Seto culture to the Finno-Ugric peoples. In 2021 Abja-Paluoja will hold the title of the Finno-Ugric Capital of Culture. In two weeks' time, we will be holding another World Congress of Finno-Ugric Peoples in the Estonian National Museum, which bears our cultural heritage in Tartu, with the aim of developing and protecting the culture and languages of Finno-Ugric peoples and promoting cooperation between them. Honourable Members of the Riigikogu! I would like to speak separately about the welfare of the libraries of Estonia. In 2018, an important study was carried out among museums and library visitors and non-visitors. It turned out that 50% of all Estonian people visited libraries. Today, the library is no longer simply a book repository and its performance cannot be measured by the number of loans. Libraries have become an information, cultural and social environment, at the heart of the community. It is certainly important that the ever-expanding possibilities of the digital world do not reduce the possibilities of the library as a meeting place. It can be said that both this year and next year are very important in rethinking the role of libraries. A draft amendment to the People's Library Act and other related laws is being prepared at the Ministry of Culture. The most recent substantial changes to the law were made in 2007. Since the information society has evolved rapidly since then, the need for information and administrative-territorial distribution and organisation of the population has changed, the current law is no longer flexible enough to develop the network of libraries and to improve the quality of the services provided. Changes to the law will help national libraries to bring their services and infrastructure into line with the requirements of public services after administrative reform and with the needs of the information society and, in particular, of young people. 2022 is the theme year of libraries. More attention will certainly help to highlight the importance of libraries both at regional level and more widely as conservationists and developers of Estonian culture. The main leader of the year is the Estonian National Library, which will present its plans in the next few months. During the last six years, Estonian film art also received attention and impetus, and during these years the landscape has changed considerably. Since 2014, the state has increased its support for the film industry. While in 2014 the Ministry of Culture, Cultural Endowment and the Estonian Film Institute supported the industry with a total of 6.95 million euros, in 2019 the grant was 13.25 million euros and in 2020 crisis assistance was added. For the sector, 2018 was the year in which a successful film programme was implemented on the occasion of the 100th anniversary of the Republic. The State's additional funding allowed the production of larger film projects, such as Truth and Justice. A total of five films, one full-length animated film, a documentary tape and a drama series came to the cinema thanks to the "Estonian Republic 100" programme. The Film Estonia support scheme, which has been implemented since 2016, facilitates the flow of foreign capital into Estonia and cooperation between Estonian and foreign film producers in the production of audiovisual works in Estonia. The scheme is funded by the Ministry of Culture and amounts to EUR 2 million per year. The production costs of audiovisual works produced in Estonia in cooperation between Estonian and foreign film producers will be supported, depending on the type of audiovisual work and the volume of costs, by 20% to 30%. The grant scheme was implemented last year as a flagship project, and as a result each euro granted in support brought back 3.32 euros to Estonia, which was spent in Estonia and thus generates additional tax revenue for the Estonian state. The attractiveness of the Estonian Film Estonia support programme as well as its profitability was confirmed by the partial film of Christopher Nolan's great film "Tene" in Tallinn in 2019. Between 2014 and 2019, the number of cinema visits in Estonia increased from 2.6 million to 3.69 million. The target was 2016 as the Estonian Pillifond was established in cooperation between the private sector and the state, whose main objective was to increase the international competitiveness of the Estonian language instrument culture and support the career of musicians. By the end of 2020, the Estonian Gill Fund had nine precious string instruments with a total value of over 1.5 million euros. The music sector was also stimulated by the celebration of the "Estonian Republic 100" anniversary, which brought about a share of additional funding already in 2017 and helped music organisations and artists to carry out more artistically ambitious projects than usual. The review of the past period cannot go past the Arvo Pärt Centre opened in 2018. Culture cannot be carried out without buildings and a number of important projects were carried out during the 20142020 period. The new building of the Estonian National Museum opened in 2016 on the Tartu Raad was certainly of the greatest importance. In addition, smaller investments and repair equipment in various parts of Estonia have been supported. I certainly consider it very important that large investments take into account the regional aspect and that the decisions taken support both access to culture and the balanced development of culture. In 2016, the government set the goal of creating Estonian language houses in Narva and Tallinn. Language houses now provide a complex service for language learning and integration, organise and coordinate Estonian language learning, support and develop the acquisition of the Estonian language and practical opportunities to use the language. People's interest is high and in 2020 free learning opportunities expanded from 2,500 to 5500. Recent integration monitoring confirms that Estonian language skills have increased and Estonian is considered important. The last six years will also include the transformation of heritage conservation activities, the adoption of the new Heritage Protection Act and the change of the role of the Heritage Protection Board. Another important step in the field of heritage conservation is the digitisation of cultural heritage. The cultural heritage programme was completed in 2018 in cooperation with the Ministry of Culture, the Ministry of Economic Affairs and Communications and the Ministry of Education and Research. Its aim is to make one third of the cultural heritage stored in Estonian memory institutions digitally available by 2023 and to upgrade the information storage infrastructure of memory institutions. In 2021, the preparation of the next action plan on digitalising and making available cultural heritage will be discussed. It is also important to talk about cooperation with the private sector, as culture is expensive and public finances are always less than needs. The Ministry of Culture thanks individuals and organisations who have supported culture financially over the years, or also through acts. A total of 209 approvals were distributed between 2014 and 2020. There have also been multiple nominations among these people. It is particularly positive that many cooperation relations are long-term and that the promotion of culture is increasingly seen as a shared task and objective between the state and the private sector. The crisis highlighted the need for such an approach for the private sector, and the greatest recognition belongs to all those who, despite uncertain times, have stayed with their partners. To sum it up. The years 2014 to 2020 have been favourable to Estonian culture and the state as a whole. The new initiatives presented to you will allow us to hope that culture will no longer only need resources, but will also be an important driver of added value and of the economy itself. Estonian culture is viable. We have both cultural participants and cultural creators everywhere in Estonia, in all age groups and in all areas. We face challenges in the field of culture, but with joint work and commitment, we can deal with them. We need to become increasingly aware that at a time when our country is celebrating the 30th anniversary of the restoration of independence, culture is the responsibility of all of us and the cause and purpose of our being our country, the work and care we all share. Thank you for listening.
